                                   industry
                               ▪   support future opportunities in the resources industry sector.

                                                   OFFICIAL
Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $54.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26
▪       $72.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.
This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach
Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed the specified funding amount would encompass administrative
costs.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding and profile specified in the election
commitment.

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                             2
                                                 OFFICIAL
Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by $830.0 million across 2022–23 to
2032–33. This is entirely due to a decrease in operating expenses.
This cost estimate is sensitive to the availability of administrative savings in the general government
sector. We have not assessed the extent to which this policy would impact on Victorian Government
service delivery.




Our approach
Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:
1. Consistent with previous budgets, administrative savings would be achieved through measures such
   as the general efficiency dividend.
2. The timing of these savings was based on the information specified in the costing request.

Method

We based this policy costing on the amount of administrative savings in 2026–27 specified in the
election commitment.

        Publicly-announced policy costing

Double Victoria's exports

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement            1 July 2023

Policy conclusion              30 June 2027

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed
                               $40.0 million towards initiatives set with a target to double Victoria’s
                               exports by 2035. These initiatives include:
                               ▪   more export assistance to small and medium businesses
                               ▪   reintroducing an outgoing Trade Mission Program
                               ▪   encouraging greater participation in export markets by Victorian
                                   Migrant Communities
                               ▪   establishing new trade and investment facilitation agreements
                                   with nations and sub-national jurisdictions
                               ▪   reviewing the location of Global Victoria Offices
                               ▪   encouraging local governments to better focus on trade
                                   opportunities for their businesses.

OFFICIAL




        Publicly-announced policy costing

Drawdown of budgeted contingencies

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement            1 January 2023

Policy conclusion              Ongoing

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to
                               drawing down all the output and asset contingencies ‘Funding not
                               allocated to specific purposes’ in the Victorian Pre-Election Budget
                               Update 2022 and in 2026–27, to fund other commitments.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or budget
                               update.
                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Drawdown of budgeted contingencies



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                 2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

There are 2 types of contingencies in the Victorian budget:

1. Funding not allocated to specific purposes – funding set aside for future government decisions.

2. Decisions made but not yet allocated – funding already earmarked for specific projects, but not yet
   allocated to government departments.

These types of contingencies exist for output and assets, making a total of 4.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $4,700 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   a decrease in operating expenses of $2,300.0 million due to a drawdown on unallocated
            operating expenses

        -   a decrease in assets of $2,400.0 million due to a drawdown on unallocated asset investment

▪       $7,600 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   a decrease in operating expenses of $3,700.0 million due to a drawdown on unallocated
            operating expenses

        -   a decrease in assets of $3,900.0 million due to a drawdown on unallocated asset investment.




Our approach

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       determined the amounts of contingencies allocated to the output and asset contingency ‘Funding
        not allocated to specific purposes’

▪       estimated the budget impact of drawing down this funding for other commitments.

Data sources

▪       Department of Treasury and Finance. 2022. 2022 Victorian Pre-Election Budget Update. [ONLINE]
        Available at: https://www.dtf.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/document/2022%20Victorian%20Pre-
        Election%20Budget%20Update.pdf. [Accessed 19 December 2022]

▪       Information provided by the Department of Treasury and Finance

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

A supply charge is the amount a household must pay to an energy retailer for being continuously
connected to an electricity network. The supply charge does not change based on the amount of
energy consumed. Rather, the supply charge is a fixed rate, in cents per day, applied over the billing
period.

There are concessions available to households to reduce the cost of the supply charge, including the:

▪       annual electricity concession

▪       medical cooling concession

▪       service to property concession

▪       excess electricity concession.

The service to property concession applies when the cost of electricity use on a bill is lower than the
service charge.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $505.6 million across 2022–23
to 2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from offsetting household
electricity supply charges, partially offset by some households no longer receiving the service to
property concession.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in the:

▪       number of electricity account holders

▪       number of electricity concession holders

▪       average supply charge rate.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The number of residential electricity account holders and concession holders would increase in line
   with forecast population growth.

2. The electricity supply charge would remain in line with its historical average.

3. Medical cooling concession holders also receive the annual electricity concession.

Method

When costing this policy, we:




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                                       Electricity bill buster


▪   determined the total number of residential electricity account holders, separate by concession and
    non-concession account holders

▪   estimated the total annual supply charge for non-concession account holders by applying the
    number of accounts to the daily rate in cents per day from 1 January 2023 to 30 June 2023

▪   estimated the total annual supply charge for concession account holders by applying the number
    of accounts to the concessional daily rate in cents per day from 1 January 2023 to 30 June 2023

▪   offset costs by the savings generated by households no longer receiving the service to property
    concession.

We applied a part year impact to the first year of this policy to reflect a proposed 1 January 2023
commencement date.

Data sources

▪   Department of Families, Fairness and Housing. 2022. Annual electricity concession. [ONLINE]
    Available at: https://services.dffh.vic.gov.au/annual-electricity-concession [Accessed 19 December
    2022]

▪   Department of Families, Fairness and Housing. 2022. Excess electricity concession. [ONLINE]
    Available at: https://services.dffh.vic.gov.au/excess-electricity-concession [Accessed 19 December
    2022]

▪   Department of Families, Fairness and Housing. 2022. Medical cooling concession. [ONLINE]
    Available at: https://services.dffh.vic.gov.au/medical-cooling-concession [Accessed 19 December
    2022]

▪   Department of Families, Fairness and Housing. 2022. Service to property charge concession.
    [ONLINE] Available at: https://services.dffh.vic.gov.au/service-property-charge-concession
    [Accessed 19 December 2022]

▪   Information provided by the Department of Families, Fairness and Housing

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $3,723.8 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $5,089.6 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to a decrease in operating expenses from the introduction of fiscal reforms to
generate savings in the general government sector, excluding health.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       changes in general government sector expenditure to 2026–27

▪       the availability of administrative savings in the general government sector.

We have not assessed the extent to which this policy would impact on Victorian Government service
delivery.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Consistent with previous budgets, administrative savings would be achieved through measures
   such as the general efficiency dividend.

2. General government sector expenditure and health sector expenditure would increase in line with
   growth in nominal GSP beyond 2025–26.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       identified existing budgeted forecast general government sector expenditure to 2025–26

▪       identified existing budgeted forecast health sector expenditure to 2025–26 and deducted these
        estimates from the total general government sector estimates

▪       estimated total general government sector expenditure excluding health expenditure in 2026–27 by
        escalating expenditure in line with nominal GSP

▪       calculated the fiscal reform savings based on the election commitment.

Data sources

▪       Department of Treasury and Finance. 2022. 2022 Victorian Pre-Election Budget Update. [ONLINE]
        Available at: https://www.dtf.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/document/2022%20Victorian%20Pre-
        Election%20Budget%20Update.pdf. [Accessed 15 December 2022]

        Publicly-announced policy costing

Help small business

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement            1 January 2023

Policy conclusion              Ongoing

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to:
                               ▪   $140.0 million to deliver a new Small Business Victoria, a new
                                   jobs, skills and growth fund and a new small business expert
                                   mentoring service
                               ▪   $30.0 million to deliver a shopping strip revitalisation program to
                                   support local small businesses, traders’ associations and the
                                   Chamber of Commerce
                               ▪   seek to freeze all existing local government business fees,
                                   charges and levies, and prevent the introduction of new ones for
                                   4 years
                               ▪   establish a special economic zone in East Werribee, where
                                   businesses established in this zone would:
                                   –   pay zero payroll tax
                                   -   be able to apply directly to the planning minister for
                                       expedited planning approvals.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or
                               budget update.
                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Help small business



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                  2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $161.1 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $157.5 million due to providing funding for small business

        -   a decrease in revenue of $3.6 million due to a reduction in payroll tax revenue

▪       $184.4 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $170.0 million due to providing funding for small business

        -   a decrease in revenue of $14.4 million due to a reduction in payroll tax revenue.

The payroll tax component cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       changes in the outlook for employment and wages growth in Victoria

▪       the number of businesses that currently pay payroll tax that would move to the East Werribee
        special economic zone.

The financial implications of the grant funding components of this policy are certain as they relate to
providing specified capped funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified
to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Taxable wages would increase in line with forecast wage growth and employment growth.

2. Moving costs and limited land availability within the East Werribee special economic zone would
   result in mostly new employers, rather than existing employers, moving to the zone.

3. The capped grant funding amounts would encompass administrative costs.



Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       projected payroll tax revenue for businesses that are liable for payroll tax in the 3030 postcode over
        the medium term

▪       estimated the impact of zero payroll tax for businesses that would choose to move to the East

                                                     OFFICIAL
Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $41.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26
▪       $53.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.
This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses due to funding toward:
▪       the Agriculture Futures Extension Program
▪       the Birchip Cropping Group’s Nexus project
▪       Victorian soil research.
The legislative changes to introduce the labelling requirements would be met within existing resources
and current food safety auditors would be responsible for compliance enforcement. We expect:
▪       this component of the policy would have no impact on the state’s budgeted net position
▪       private businesses would incur any costs associated with complying with the new regulations.
The financial implications of the other components of this policy are certain as they relate to specified
capped funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the
desired outcomes.


Our approach
Assumptions

1. Administrative costs would be absorbed by the relevant department.
2. Funding would be a one-off grant to Birchip Cropping Group, which would undertake the works.
3. Funding to the Agriculture Futures Extension Program would be provided as grants evenly over
   2023−24 to 2026−27.
4. Funding to Victorian soil research would be provided as grants evenly over 2023−24 to 2032−33.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding specified in the election commitment.

                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $1,332.9 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   a decrease in operating expenses of $1,213.7 million due to savings from reprioritising
            budgeted initiatives

        -   a decrease in assets of $119.2 million due to savings from ceasing budgeted asset works.

▪       $1,532.9 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   a decrease in operating expenses of $1,413.7 million due to savings from reprioritising
            budgeted initiatives

        -   a decrease in assets of $119.2 million due to savings from ceasing budgeted asset works.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in amounts already spent and contractually committed as at
1 January 2023.

For some projects, the financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped
funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of these funding amounts specified to achieve the
desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Funds already spent would not be recoverable.

2. Existing contractual commitments would be honoured under this policy.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       determined the amount of funding committed to the project in the budget

▪       where possible, determined the amount of funding expected to be spent to 1 January 2023

▪       estimated the amount of funds already spent

▪       where possible, determined the value and termination costs of existing contracts

▪       calculated the amount of funds the policy would save.

Data sources

▪       Information provided by the Department of Premier and Cabinet

Respect for rural communities

Political party        Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader   The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement    1 July 2023

Policy conclusion      Ongoing

Policy summary         On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to:
                       ▪   review Victoria’s public land management standards to ensure ‘good
                           neighbour’ relations that put equivalent standards onto both the
                           government and private landholders

                       ▪   transfer management from Parks Victoria to the Game Management
                           Authority (GMA) of State Game Reserves (SGR) where the GMA can
                           prove it is better suited to manage that SGR for the benefit of the
                           environment and the Victorian community

                       ▪   strengthen Victoria’s farm trespass laws to bring maximum fines for
                           individuals in line with NSW and to include licenced water frontage in
                           farmer’s biosecurity management plan

                       ▪   amend the Land Act 1958 to achieve a more balanced approach to
                           camping on crown land river frontage property by creating an opt-in
                           system for campers to seek permission of land holders, increasing
                           penalties for breaches of biosecurity measures and increasing the
                           powers of authorised officers to enforce this new approach

                       ▪   manage Victoria’s brumby population through re-homing and veterinary
                           intervention, and end the plan to eradicate brumbies through aerial and
                           ground shooting – met from within current department funding

                       ▪   ensure that all proposed legislation is reviewed to assess its
                           compatibility, and potential conflict, with neighbouring state
                           jurisdictions and its impact on cross-border communities, and support
                           the ongoing resourcing of the Victorian Cross Border Commissioner
                           and change the role to a Governor-in-Council appointment.

                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $1.5 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from the public land management
review and the proposed legislation review.

We expect the policy to increase farm trespass fines would have an immaterial but positive impact on
revenue.

We do not expect the remaining elements of this policy to have an impact on the state’s budgeted net
position.

This cost estimate is sensitive to the final size and scope of the reviews.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed that funding for the public land management review and the
proposed legislation review would be based on similar comparable reviews, adjusted for growth in the
Victorian wage price index.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       determined the costs and funding profiles associated with similar reviews undertaken by Victorian
        government departments

▪       estimated the cost of a review into public land management and a review into proposed legislation
        based on these similar reviews, adjusted by the Victorian wage price index.

Data sources

        management-to-improve-vict [Accessed 11 November 2022]

▪       Victorian Budget 2019-20, Budget Paper No.3 Service Delivery 2019-20

Sewerage works lease

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement            1 July 2023

Policy conclusion              Ongoing

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to:
                               ▪   offering a 50-year lease of Melbourne Water’s sewerage treatment
                                   business, including 2 treatment plants and associated
                                   infrastructure
                               ▪   transferring the proceeds of the asset lease to the Victorian Future
                                   Fund
                               ▪   undertake a phased withdrawal of the total principal fund
                                   investment associated with the lease of Melbourne Water’s assets
                                   and related earnings from the Victorian Future Fund.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or budget
                               update.
                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Sewerage works lease



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                   2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Victorian budget and our costing response include budget impacts in the general government
sector (GGS). Melbourne Water is a public non-financial corporation (PNFC) which is outside the GGS.
The PNFC sector contains government-controlled corporations that are treated like government
subsidiaries. Melbourne Water’s activities impact the budget in a different way to government
departments.

Melbourne Water is budgeted to pay dividends to the government over the forward estimates. The
Treasurer of Victoria determines the Melbourne Water dividend after consultation with the Board of
Melbourne Water and the Minster of Water.

The government announced the Victorian Future Fund (future fund) in the 2022–23 budget. On 30 June
2022, the Premier’s website stated that $7.9 billion, generated from the VicRoads Modernisation Joint
Venture, would be invested in the future fund.

The Victorian Funds Management Corporation (VFMC) manages the future fund. The VFMC is a public
financial corporate that sits outside the general government sector.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $6,660.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in revenue of $6,668.7 million from phased drawdowns of the asset sale

        -   partly offset by a decrease in revenue of $8.7 million due to reduced dividends paid by
            Melbourne Water to the government.

▪       $6,610.5 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in revenue of $6,668.7 million from phased drawdowns of the asset sale

        -   partly offset by a decrease in revenue of $58.2 million due to reduced dividends paid by
            Melbourne Water to the government.

We estimate that a 50-year lease of Melbourne Water’s sewerage and recycled water assets would have
a value of $6,248.1 million in 2023–24.

By leasing the sewerage component of Melbourne Water, we expect that the profitability of Melbourne
Water would fall, leading to a reduction in earnings and equity. This would reduce the net worth of
Melbourne Water over the leasing period. A reduction in a PNFC’s net worth does not directly affect the
state budget.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in:

▪       the price offered for leasing the asset by private sector bidders and the number of competing
        bidders

▪       future service demand growth

▪       future Essential Services Commission (ESC) pricing decisions

▪       future expected profitability of the sewerage treatment business

▪       conditions or restrictions negotiated on the lease.




    3
                                               OFFICIAL

                                                                                   Sewerage works lease




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Melbourne Water would be split into 2 businesses:

    -   the leased business

    -   a PNFC that would manage the remaining parts of the business and the lease.

2. The private sector business that takes on the lease would pay an upfront payment for the 50-year
   lease to the PNFC, which would then transfer that money to the government.

3. A private sector business would take up the lease commencing in the 2023–24 financial year.

4. The ESC would continue to set prices for the sewerage services.

5. The drawdown of funds from the Victorian Future Fund would be recognised as revenue to the
   general government sector.

6. The reduction in dividend payments to the government would be proportional to the amount of
   revenue raised by the sewerage services.

7. The revenue generated from the lease of the business would be deposited into the Victorian Future
   Fund on 1 July 2023.

8. The Victorian Future Fund balance would be withdrawn over 3 years:

    a. 50% in 2023–24

    b. 50% in 2024–25

    c. 100% in 2025–26.

9. VFMC earnings on future fund assets would be in line with the 8-year average of VFMC’s actual
   3-year per annum returns for foundation centralised investment model (CIM) clients.

10. Withdraws would occur on 1 July each year a withdrawal is stipulated.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated the regulated asset base (RAB) and earnings before interest, tax, depreciation, and
    amortisation (EBITDA) for the sewerage component of Melbourne Water

▪   added multipliers to the RAB and EBITDA based on the results of other leases of essential services
    undertaken within Australia

▪   averaged the results of the different measures

▪   estimated the reduced dividend resulting from the leasing agreement




                                                                                                      4
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


▪       estimated the VFMC’s per annum returns

▪       estimated the Victorian Future Fund earnings from the deposit of the revenue earned from the
        lease of Melbourne Water’s assets, accounting for revenue withdrawals

▪       estimated the revenue withdrawn from the Victorian Future Fund each financial year.

                                                  Victorian Future Fund drawdown



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                             2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The government announced the Victorian Future Fund in the 2022–23 budget. On 30 June 2022, the
Premier’s website stated that $7.9 billion, generated from the VicRoads Modernisation Joint Venture,
would be invested in the fund.

The government plans to make further investments into the fund through proceeds from designated
government land sales and a proportion of future budget surpluses. The fund is budgeted to pay
dividends each year over the forward estimates.

The Victorian Funds Management Corporation (VFMC) manages the fund. The VFMC is a public
financial corporation that sits outside the general government sector.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $10,650.4 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in revenue of $10,732.4 million due to a drawdown from the fund

        -   partly offset by a decrease in revenue of $82.0 million due to dividend payments foregone.

▪       $6,781.8 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in revenue of $10,732.4 million due to a drawdown from the fund

        -   partly offset by a decrease in revenue of $3,950.6 due to dividend payments foregone.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in:

▪       VFMC portfolio returns

▪       dividend payout ratio

▪       the value and timing of dividend payments

▪       the value and timing of additional contributions to the fund.

This costing is also sensitive to the specific arrangements for the Victorian Future Fund. None of the
2022-23 Victorian Budget, 2022 Victorian economic and fiscal update or the 2022 Pre-election budget
update detailed the specific arrangements including:

▪       how the balance of the fund would be used to offset borrowings

▪       Whether the fund would be within or outside the general government sector.

We sought information from the Department of Treasury and Finance. While it provided some
information, it advised that materials relating to the Victorian Future Fund were commercially
confidential.




    3
                                               OFFICIAL

                                                                           Victorian Future Fund drawdown



Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Earnings on the fund’s assets and additional contributions would be in line with government
   estimates.

2. Fund earnings over the period 2026–27 to 2032–33 would be in line with the 8-year average of
   VFMC’s actual 3-year per annum returns for Foundation CIM clients.

3. There would be no inflows into the fund, other than retained earnings, over the period 2026–27 to
   2032–33.

4. Drawdowns would occur on 30 June each year a drawdown is stipulated.

5. The Victorian Future Fund sits outside the general government sector.

6. Dividend payments foregone over the period 2025–26 to 2032–33 would be in line with the 2025–
   26 dividend payout ratio.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   identified the government’s fund forecasts for 2025–26

▪   estimated the fund’s earnings over the period 2026–27 to 2032–33

▪   estimated the fund’s dividend payments foregone.

        Publicly-announced policy costing
Drawdown of the sustainability fund for projects that improve Victoria's
environment

Political party                Liberals and Nationals
Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP
Policy commencement            1 July 2023
Policy conclusion              30 June 2027
Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to
                               drawdown funds from the Sustainability Fund for projects that improve
                               Victoria’s environment.
Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500
Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update
Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or budget
                               update.
                                                  OFFICIAL
                             Drawdown of the sustainability fund for projects that improve Victoria's environment



Our assessment
Context

The Victorian Government collects the Municipal and Industrial Waste Levy. Some of these funds are
distributed to environmental agencies and the remainder go into the Sustainability Fund.
The Premier and the Minister for Environment and Climate Action determine how to use money in the
Sustainability Fund.

Our response

We expect this policy to have no impact on the state’s budgeted net position as money in the
Sustainability Fund is already held by the general government sector.
Any expenditure using this fund would only result in an increase in operating expenses.

OFFICIAL




        Publicly-announced policy costing

Strengthening Victoria's native timber industry and protecting old growth
forests

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement            1 January 2023

Policy conclusion              Ongoing

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to
                               immediately reverse the ban on native timber harvesting, which is
                               scheduled to commence in 2030.
                               This policy would be funded from within existing departmental
                               appropriations.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or budget
                               update.
                                                 OFFICIAL

                               Strengthening Victoria's native timber industry and protecting old growth forests



Our assessment

Context

The Victorian Government announced in November 2019 that it would phase out native forest logging
by 2030, and the logging of old growth forests would cease immediately. VicForests is the state-owned
business responsible for the harvest, commercial sale, and re-growing of timber from Victoria’s state
forests.

Our response

We expect this policy to have no impact on the state’s budgeted net position as it is specified to be
funded from existing departmental appropriations. We made no judgement on the impact to other
functions delivered by the relevant department because of this policy.

OFFICIAL




        Publicly-announced policy costing
Fixing the Planning Process

Political party                Liberals and Nationals
Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP
Policy commencement            1 July 2023
Policy conclusion              Ongoing
Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to:
                               ▪   make changes to Neighbourhood Residential Zones including
                                   introducing:
                                   -   mechanism which respects identified neighbourhood
                                       character
                                   -   requirement for the preservation of existing tree canopy
                                   -   two-dwelling maximum on a titled site
                                   -   mandatory height limits.
                               ▪   improve the protection of Victoria’s built heritage and pre-1930
                                   homes.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500
Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update
Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or
                               budget update.
                                                OFFICIAL
                                                                                   Fixing the Planning Process



Our assessment
Context

Under Victoria's planning system, local councils and the state government develop planning schemes to
control land use and development. The neighbourhood residential zone is applied to land that has been
identified as having specific neighbourhood, heritage, environmental or landscape character values that
distinguish the land from other parts of the municipality or surrounding area.
State and local government have a range of heritage protections in place, usually in the form of planning
overlays or protections for specific buildings or sites.

Our response

We expect this policy to have no impact on the state’s budgeted net position as:
▪   these policies are an adjustment or extension of existing regulations in these areas
▪   the cost of changing regulations would be covered under existing functions and departmental
    funding.

                                                                                                                2
                                                    OFFICIAL
Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Eastern Clinical Trials and Research Centre (ECTRC) is a proposed $180 million construction to be
jointly developed and operated by Eastern Health and Monash University.
Monash University’s Eastern Clinical Research Unit is part of the Eastern Health Clinical School. Eastern
Health manages Box Hill Hospital.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $104.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:
        -   an increase in assets of $173.4 million due to construction of the centre
        -   an increase in revenue of $69.4 million from the Australian Government and Monash University
▪       $108.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:
        -   an increase in assets of $180.0 million due to construction of the centre
        -   an increase in revenue of $72.0 million from the Australian Government and Monash University.
The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $180.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24 to
completion in 2027–28.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes. We made no
judgement on whether the specified co-contribution from the Australian Government would be given.




Our approach
Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:
1. ECTRC would be built as an extension of Box Hill Hospital.
2. Timelines for the construction would follow similar projects.
3. Australian Government contributions would follow construction timelines.
4. All funding would be for the construction of the centre and would not cover ongoing operational or
   maintenance costs.
5. The relevant department would absorb administration costs.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election
commitment.

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

CAD systems are utilised by dispatchers, call-takers, and triple-zero operators to prioritise and record
incident calls, identify the status and location of responders in the field, and effectively dispatch
responder personnel.
Emergency responders in the field can receive messages initiated by CAD systems via their mobile data
terminals, radios, and mobile phones.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $129.7 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:
        -   an increase in assets of $100.0 million
        -   an increase in operating expenses of $29.7 million
▪       $131.2 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:
        -   an increase in assets of $100.0 million
        -   an increase in operating expense of $31.2 million.
This is entirely due to an increase in both operating expenses and assets for funding, systems, training
and recruitment.

works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $100.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2022–23 to
completion in 2024–25.

        Publicly-announced policy costing

Free public transport for health professionals (including V/Line) and
paying paramedic registration fees

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement            1 July 2023

Policy conclusion              30 June 2027

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to free
                               public transport for more than 260,000 Victorian healthcare workers,
                               including:
                               ▪   nurses
                               ▪   personal and aged care workers
                               ▪   trainee doctors and allied health workers
                               ▪   paramedics
                               ▪   other hospital workers, like patient transport orderlies and clerical
                                   staff.
                               This policy would also provide $6.44 million to pay Victorian
                               paramedics’ annual professional registration fees for the next 4 years.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or budget
                               update.
                                                   OFFICIAL

          Free public transport for health professionals (including V/Line) and paying paramedic registration fees



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                               2
                                                     OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

This policy would make fares free for selected Victorian healthcare workers. In doing so it interacts with
the Liberals and Nationals’ publicly-announced policy costings:

▪       Cap local public transport fares to $2 full fare $1 concession fare

▪       Halve V/Line fares

The cap local public transport fares policy would reduce the daily caps from current levels to $2 for full
fare paying passengers and $1 for concession fare paying passengers. While the cap local public
transport fares policy would not apply to V/Line services, the Halve V/Line fares policy would reduce all
V/Line fares by 50%.

This policy would further reduce daily public transport fare caps to zero for affected Victorian
healthcare workers.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $207.3 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        –   a decrease in revenue of $202.5 million due to reduced farebox revenue from healthcare
            workers
        -   an increase in operating expenses of $4.8 million due to paying paramedic registration fees

▪       $281.3 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        –   a decrease in revenue of $274.8 million due to reduced farebox revenue from healthcare
            workers
        -   an increase in operating expenses of $6.4 million due to paying paramedic registration fees.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in public transport usage.

The financial implications of the policy component to pay paramedic registration fees are certain as
they relate to specified capped funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding
specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. There would be no impact on operating costs of the public transport system.

2. Public transport usage by healthcare workers would be constant.

3. Healthcare workers would take an average of 2 trips per day during routine working days.




    3
                                                   OFFICIAL

          Free public transport for health professionals (including V/Line) and paying paramedic registration fees


4. The use of V/Line services by healthcare workers would be consistent with the general uptake of
   the V/Line services.

5. Eligible healthcare population would increase in line with the historical trend.

6. 260,000 healthcare workers would be eligible for this policy in 2022–23.

Method

When costing this policy, we estimated the revenue foregone by providing free public transport for
healthcare workers after considering public transport usage, fare prices, and eligible population.

We based the operating expenditure of this policy costing on the capped amount of funding specified in
the costing request.

 5
OFFICIAL




        Publicly-announced policy costing
Future pandemic management

Political party                Liberals and Nationals
Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP
Policy commencement            1 July 2023
Policy conclusion              30 June 2024
Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to:
                               ▪   amend the Victorian Constitution Act 1975 to limit a State of
                                   Emergency or State of Disaster declaration and to ensure that the
                                   operation of the Parliament cannot be suspended under a State of
                                   Emergency or State of Disaster declaration
                               ▪   repeal the 2021 pandemic legislation and replace it with more
                                   considered laws
                               ▪   guarantee no more lockdowns.
Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500
Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update
Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or budget
                               update.
                                                 OFFICIAL
                                                                               Future pandemic management



Our assessment
Context

During the COVID-19 pandemic, the government enacted a State of Emergency and periodically renewed
it with a non-renewable expiration date of 15 December 2021. This was mandated by the Public Health &
Wellbeing Act 2008. The Act was subsequently amended to broaden the power of the Victorian
Government during pandemics to effectively transfer the power to make pandemic orders, from the Chief
Health Officer to the Premier of Victoria and the Minister of Health.

Our response

We expect this policy to have no impact on the state’s budgeted net position as:
▪   the cost of amending the Victorian Constitution Act 1975 is likely incorporated in the operational
    budget of the Department of Justice and Community Safety
▪   the State of Emergency had a non-renewable expiration date of 15 December 2021
▪   the cost of repealing the pandemic laws through the removal of Part 8A of the Public Health &
    Wellbeing Act 2008 is likely incorporated in the operational budget of the Department of Justice and
    Community Safety.

                                                    OFFICIAL
Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $198.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:
        -   an increase in operating expenses of $360.0 million due to increased funding for elective surgery
        -   partly offset by an increase in revenue of $162.0 million due to increased funding from the
            Australian Government.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes. We made no
judgement on whether the specified co-contributions from the Australian Government would be given.




Our approach
Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:
1. The specified funding amount would encompass administrative costs.
2. Consistent with the Addendum to National Health Funding Reform 2020–2025 the Victorian
   Government would provide 55% of the additional hospital funding and the Australian Government
   would provide 45%.

Method

When costing this policy, we:
▪       estimated the Australian Government funds provided as revenue to the Victorian Government
▪       derived the Victorian Government funding contribution.

        -   an increase in assets of $3,983.2 million due to capital works, partly offset by
        -   an increase in revenue of $1,395.3 million from the Australian and New South Wales
            governments
▪       $6,526.8 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:
        -   an increase in assets of $10,189.8 million due to capital works, partly offset by
        -   an increase in revenue of $3,663.0 million from the Australian and New South Wales
            governments.
The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $10,189.8 million from the commencement of asset works in 2022–23
to completion in 2029–30.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes. We made no
judgement on whether the specified co-contributions from the Australian Government would be given




Our approach
Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed that the specified funding amount would encompass
administrative costs.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election
commitment.

OFFICIAL




        Publicly-announced policy costing
Provide more affordable IVF and egg freezing

Political party                Liberals and Nationals
Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP
Policy commencement            1 July 2023
Policy conclusion              Ongoing
Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed:
                               ▪   $165.5 million for:
                                   –   a rebate of up to $2,000 to undertake an initial IVF procedure
                                       using private fertility clinics
                                   –   a rebate of up to $2,000 to undertake a subsequent IVF
                                       procedure (if not successful with the initial procedure)
                                   –   rebates of up to $500 for pre–IVF Fertility Testing
                                   –   up to 5 days of paid fertility treatment leave for nurses,
                                       teachers, and other Victorian public service workers
                               ▪   $21.0 million for a rebate of up to $7,000 to Victorian women
                                   with medical conditions such as severe endometriosis and
                                   cancers that require chemotherapy, to ensure they can freeze
                                   their eggs without significant out-of-pocket expense.
Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500
Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update
Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or
                               budget update.
                                       OFFICIAL
                                                  Provide more affordable IVF and egg freezing



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                           2
                                                    OFFICIAL
Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

In vitro fertilisation (IVF) is a procedure, where an egg and sperm are joined together outside the body, to
overcome a range of fertility issues. A single IVF cycle currently costs $10,532, with an estimated out-of-
pocket cost of $5,483 for an initial cycle and $4,898 for a subsequent cycle due to Medicare rebates.
Under this policy, individuals working in the public sector would be eligible for paid fertility treatment
leave. The public sector is made up of the Victorian Public Service (VPS) and employees working for
Victorian public entities, which include public health services, schools, TAFE, police and emergency
services, water and land management, creative industries, finance, and transport.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $141.6 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26
▪       $186.5 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.
This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from funding egg freezing, IVF and fertility
testing rebates, and providing paid fertility leave to Victorian public sector employees.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach
Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed the specified funding amount would encompass administrative
costs.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election
commitment.

government revenue and expenses, and movements in financial and non-financial assets. It is an indicator of the policy’s impact on
the state budget, and is more appropriate for comparing policy costings. A positive change indicates that this policy would improve
the net position of the state budget.

        Publicly-announced policy costing

First home owner stamp duty relief and 150,000 new housing lots

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Policy conclusion              30 June 2027

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed
                               $45.5 million to improve housing affordability by:
                               ▪   facilitating the release of 150,000 new housing lots
                               ▪   introducing planning and building reforms to make approvals
                                   faster
                               ▪   introducing new apprentice travel funds
                               ▪   introducing a new local development infrastructure fund.
                               For 2023 only, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP also committed to
                               increasing the eligibility thresholds for land transfer duty reductions
                               for first home buyers, so that properties with a dutiable value of
                               $1,000,000 or less would be exempt from land transfer duty.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or budget
                               update.
                                          OFFICIAL

                                       First home owner stamp duty relief and 150,000 new housing lots



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                   2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Land transfer duty, Victoria's largest source of state tax revenue, is levied on property transactions. The
amount of duty paid by purchasers primarily depends on the dutiable value of the property and how the
property will be used.

Several exemptions and concessions apply, with the largest being for first home buyers. Eligible first
home buyers of properties with a dutiable value of $600,000 or less are currently exempt from land
transfer duty. Eligible first home buyers of properties with a dutiable value between $600,000 and
$750,000 currently receive a concession from land transfer duty. Other exemptions and concessions to
land transfer duty include:

▪       off-the-plan concession

▪       pensioner duty exemption or concession

▪       principal place of residence concession

▪       young farmer exemption or concession.

Purchasers may qualify for more than one of these exemptions and concessions. Some purchasers can
claim multiple exemptions and concessions. Other purchasers must choose which exemptions and
concessions they would like to apply

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $296.3 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   a decrease in revenue of $260.8 million due to foregone land transfer duty

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $35.5 million due to funding for the other housing
            affordability components of this policy

▪       $306.3 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   a decrease in revenue of $260.8 million due to foregone land transfer duty

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $45.5 million due to funding for the other housing
            affordability components of this policy.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in the function and behaviour of the property market,
including:

▪       dwelling demand, supply constraints, interest rates and prices which impact transaction volumes

▪       other government policies.

Apart from the land transfer duty component of this policy, the financial implications of this policy are
certain, as they relate to specified capped funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the
funding specified to achieve these desired outcomes.




    3
                                                 OFFICIAL

                                              First home owner stamp duty relief and 150,000 new housing lots




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. For 2023 only, eligible first home buyers of properties with a dutiable value between $1,000,000 and
   $1,150,000 would receive a concession from land transfer duty.

2. Victorian Government revenue foregone due to other land transfer duty exemptions and
   concessions would not be affected.

3. The first home owner grant would not be affected.

4. The property market would not be affected, including:

    a. demand from first home buyers

    b. supply of qualifying properties

    c. price of dwellings

    d. transaction volumes.

5. The proportion of first home buyers to all buyers of principal places of residence is not price
   sensitive and would remain consistent over time.

6. Land transfer duty revenue would continue to grow at the budgeted rate beyond the period of the
   forward estimates. In the absence of this policy, the total value of land transfer duty exemptions
   and concessions for first home buyers would also grow at this rate.

7. State Revenue Office administration and compliance costs would not be affected.

8. The specified funding amount would encompass administrative costs for the following policy
   components:

    a. facilitating the release of 150,000 new housing lots

    b. introducing planning and building reforms to make approvals faster

    c. introducing new apprentice travel funds

    d. introducing a new local development infrastructure fund.

Method

When costing the land transfer duty component of this policy, we:

▪   estimated the value of land transfer duty exemptions and concessions received by eligible first
    home buyers

▪   estimated the value of land transfer duty potentially applicable to principal places of residence if
    there were no duty exemptions and concessions




                                                                                                           4
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


▪       estimated the proportion of potential land transfer duty revenue that is reduced by the first home
        buyer duty exemption and concession

▪       estimated the cost of the first home buyer duty exemption and concession at different property
        value thresholds

▪       calculated the reduction in land transfer duty revenue caused by an increase in the first home buyer
        duty exemption and concession thresholds

For the other components of this policy, we used the capped amount of funding and phasing specified
in the election commitment.

Costing overview

        -   an increase in assets of $31.0 million

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $132.6 million
        -   an increase in assets of $31.0 million.
The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $31.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2024–25 to
completion in 2026–27.
We expect the policies to reinstate Victoria’s move on laws, bring forward the Swastika ban, and to
undertake a review to overhaul Victoria’s remand system and reduce the number of prisoners in custody,
would be day-to-day government business and would not result in an impact to the state budget.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.

costs.

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $37.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:
        -   an increase in assets of $25.0 million due to rejuvenating Victoria’s aged care facilities
        -   an increase in operating expenses of $12.0 million from dispute resolution services, establishing
            funds and a program.
▪       $69.3 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:
        -   an increase in assets of $50.0 million due to rejuvenating Victoria’s aged care facilities
        -   an increase in operating expenses of $19.3 million from funding for Victoria’s Neighbourhood
            Houses, dispute resolution services, establishing funds and a program.
The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $50.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2025–26 to
completion in 2026–27.
We expect that the following policies would have no impact on the state’s budgeted net position:
▪       new retirement village legislation
▪       establish an agency within the Department of Housing to regulate rooming houses
▪       consolidate all rooming house regulation into the new Rooming Houses Act.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach
Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:
1. Funding would be provided as grants to Victoria’s Neighbourhood Houses, statewide carer support
   groups, aged care providers and the Dispute Settlement Centre of Victoria.
2. Funding would be provided for the upgrade of Victoria’s aged care facilities, rather than ongoing
   operational or maintenance costs.
3. The specified funding amount would encompass administrative costs.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding specified in the election commitment,
and the proposed profile for the operational expenditure and asset investment.

OFFICIAL




        Publicly-announced policy costing

Cut seven Labor taxes

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement            1 January 2023

Policy conclusion              Ongoing

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to:
                               ▪   from 1 July 2023:
                                   –   abolishing the commercial passenger vehicle service levy
                                   –   unwinding the increase in building practitioner fees
                                       implemented by the Victorian Building Authority on 2 May
                                       2022
                                   –   making all transfers of property between spouses exempt from
                                       land transfer duty
                                   –   reducing legal certificate fees to the level payable prior to
                                       March 2022
                                   –   raising the annual payroll tax-free threshold to $1.6 million
                                       from 2027–28, with a phased increase from 2023–24, applying
                                       a linearly increasing payroll tax rate from zero to the existing
                                       rates for each dollar between $1.6 million and $2.0 million, and
                                       imposing a maximum annual payroll tax liability deduction of
                                       $700,000
                                   –   pausing the zero and low-emission vehicle distance-based
                                       charge until 2027
                                   –   changing the land tax exemption for charitable organisations
                                       from land used and occupied exclusively for charitable
                                       purposes to land used exclusively for charitable purposes
                               ▪   from 1 January 2023, providing $6.75 million over 5 years to
                                   protect charities against the impact of the landfill levy.
                               Appendix B lists the payroll tax thresholds from 2023–24 to 2027–28
                               under this policy.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or budget
                               update.
                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Cut seven Labor taxes



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                    2
                                                        OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Commercial passenger vehicle service levy
The commercial passenger vehicle service levy (the levy) started on 1 July 2018 and applies to every
taxi and hire car trip, including ride share, in Victoria. In the 2016–17 Budget Update, the levy was
slated to be $2 but was reduced to $1 when legislated. The levy increased on 1 July 2022 to $1.05.

Building practitioner fees
The Victorian Building Authority (VBA) is the chief regulator of Victoria’s building industry. It is largely
self-funded through registration and licensing fees from the building sector and state government
grants. It also received grant funding from the Department of Environment, Land, Water and Planning
and reports to the Minister for Planning. In 2020–21, $49.5 million of it $140.3 million revenue came
from state government grants.

The Minister for Planning provides a statement to the VBA every 2 years outlining expectations and
performance targets for the authority. In 2021, the Minister for Planning set a target for the VBA to
improve its financial sustainability and complete a fee model review by 31 March 2022. As a result, the
VBA changed the structure of its building practitioner fees from 2 May 2022 to ensure financial
self-sufficiency. This changed individual and corporate registration, annual and renewal fees, and
means higher fees for some registration types.

Land transfer duty exemption for property transfers between spouses
All property transfers between spouses or domestic partners were exempt from land transfer duty prior
to 1 July 2017. From 2017–18 onwards, only transfers of principal place of residence properties
between spouses or domestic partners have been exempt from land transfer duty.

Legal certificate fees
On 1 March 2022, the fee for a legal certificate for births, deaths and marriages registration increased:

▪       from $34.30 (2.28 fee units)

▪       to $51.40 (3.42 fee units).

Payroll tax
Employers are liable for payroll tax in Victoria if they:

▪       pay wages in Victoria

▪       have an Australian wage bill above the Victorian payroll tax threshold

▪       are not eligible for a payroll tax exemption.

The payroll tax threshold is the same for all Victorian employers, however there are different rates for
employers in metropolitan and regional areas. Regional employers are those that pay at least 85% of
their total Australian wages bill to employees based in regional Victoria.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                                      Cut seven Labor taxes


                                Current payroll tax thresholds and rates

Payroll tax                                                                            2021–22 onward

Threshold                                                                                       $700,000

Metropolitan rate                                                                                   4.85%

Regional rate                                                                                    1.2125%

Source: State Revenue Office.


Electric vehicles (EV) tax
From 1 July 2021, the Zero and Low Emission Vehicle Distance-based Charge Act 2021 (the Act) imposed a
charge on zero and low emission vehicles (ZLEV). Vehicles covered by the Act include electric vehicles
(EV), hydrogen vehicles (HEV) and plug-in hybrid electrical vehicles (PHEV). Different rates are charged
on EV and HEV compared to PHEV, and the Victorian Government increases rates each year based on
the Melbourne consumer price index.

Land tax exemption for charities
Currently, the Victorian Government provides a land tax exemption for land used and occupied by
charities exclusively for charitable purposes. The definition was changed following a Supreme Court
ruling, which under the previous criteria, allowed Melbourne University to be exempt from paying tax on
land leased to a private company that was used for a student accommodation facility. The budget
forecast cost of this tax exemption did not change following the change in exemption definition, as the
intent of the exemption did not change.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪   $510.9 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

    -   a decrease in revenue of $505.7 million due to a reduction in tax revenue

    -   an increase in operating expenses of $5.3 million due to funding to charities for protection
        against the landfill levy

▪   $3,164.9 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

    -   a decrease in revenue of $3,158.2 million due to a reduction in tax revenue

    -   an increase in operating expenses of $6.8 million due to funding to charities for protection
        against the landfill levy.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in the values of taxes and levies, the quantity of underlying
drivers for all taxes, and macroeconomic forecasts. There is insufficient information available to
estimate the reduction in land tax collected due to the exemption on land used exclusively for
charitable purposes but not owned by a charitable institution, however, we expect this policy
component would have no material impact on the budget.




                                                                                                           4
                                                OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing



Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The commercial passenger vehicle service levy would increase in line with the Melbourne
   consumer price index from 2023–24 onwards under current policy settings.

2. The number of commercial passenger trips would increase in line with population growth.

3. Building practitioner fee revenue would increase in line with the Melbourne consumer price index
   and the Victorian population.

4. Land transfer duty revenue from the transfer of non-principal place of residence properties between
   spouses and domestic partners would increase in line with:

     a. aggregate land transfer duty revenue from 2022–23 to 2025–26

     b. Victorian real gross state product and the Melbourne consumer price index from 2026–27
        onwards.

5. The number of legal certificates issued in Victoria would increase in line with the Victorian
   population.

6. Legal certificate fees would increase in line with the Melbourne consumer price index.

7. Under existing payroll tax arrangements, the number of businesses liable for payroll tax would grow
   steadily between 2022–23 and 2032–33, reflecting the impact of the nominally fixed tax-free
   threshold.

8. The distribution of businesses between regional and metropolitan Victoria would remain stable
   between 2022–23 and 2032–33.

9. Taxable wages would increase in line with forecast wage growth and employment growth.

10. The change to Victoria’s payroll tax revenues would not have a material impact on the Australian
    Government’s distribution of GST revenues to Victoria.

11. Behavioural response from employers, such as changing hiring practices, would not materially
    impact payroll tax revenue.

12. The average annual distance travelled by electric and hybrid vehicles would remain the same.

13. The proportion of Australian vehicles registered in Victoria would remain the same.

14. The proportion of PHEV in total ZLEV sales would remain the same.

15. The growth in ZLEV sales in Victoria would be constant between years projected in the Australian
    Department of Industry, Science, Energy and Resources Australia’s Emissions Projections 2021
    report (2020–21, 2025–26, and 2032–33).




 5
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                                     Cut seven Labor taxes


Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated tax revenue for all taxes under existing policy settings

▪   calculated the reduction in revenue from the proposed changes to policy settings

▪   based the funding to charities for protection against the landfill levy on the capped amount of
    funding and phasing specified in the election commitment.
