                                                  $8 billion for 29 level crossing removals



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                        2
                                                     OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Victorian Government plans to remove 110 road-rail level crossings across Melbourne by 2030.
Asset works include building new railway stations, track duplication and train stabling yards.

The Victorian Government has committed funding for level crossing removal projects across multiple
budgets. Total estimated investment for existing budget initiatives is:

▪       $6,343.7 million for the ‘Level Crossing Removal Program’ initiative, which includes $151.0 million
        of Australian Government funding

▪       $8,946.0 million for the ‘85 by 2025 (Level Crossing Removal)’ initiative

▪       $6,522.1 million for the ‘Twenty-five more level crossing removals by 2030’ initiative.

This policy would rescope and rephase the existing ‘Twenty-five more level crossing removals by 2030’
initiative, funding and prioritising the additional asset works proposed in this policy, while delaying
other level crossing removal project asset works. All currently funded level crossing removal project
asset works would be completed by 2031.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $99.6 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, reflecting an increase in operating expenses due to
        ongoing maintenance costs and a one-off contract variation.

▪       $1,851.8 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $1,510.5 million due to level crossing removal project works

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $341.3 due to ongoing maintenance costs and a one-off
            contract variation.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $1,510.5 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2031–32.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       the final design of each level crossing removal and new railway station

▪       changes in cost escalation rates

▪       the terms and conditions of level crossing removal project contracts.




    3
                                                 OFFICIAL

                                                                      $8 billion for 29 level crossing removals




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Construction costs and profile would be consistent with similar projects.

2. There would be no relocation of utilities.

3. Funds already spent would not be recoverable.

4. Contract variation costs would be a proportion of the project’s remaining funding amount.

5. Contract variation costs would be proportionately similar to termination costs associated with
   relevant components of a comparable project.

6. The Australian Government funded feasibility study into removing the level crossing at Tooronga
   Road, Malvern would be completed before policy commencement.

7. The Australian Government has rescinded its funding commitment of $260.0 million for removing
   the level crossing at Glenferrie Road, Kooyong.

8. There would be no change in the amount of rail passenger services.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined which level crossings the policy would remove

▪   determined the amount of funding committed to level crossing removal initiatives in the budget

▪   estimated the profile of the level crossing removal initiatives in the budget

▪   estimated the cost and profile of delivering the prioritised asset works

▪   estimated the cost of varying existing project contracts

▪   estimated the ongoing operations and maintenance costs associated with the upgraded road and
    rail infrastructure

▪   estimated the cost and profile of delivering the rephased asset works.

Publicly-announced policy costing

 3
                                                  OFFICIAL

                                                             An integrated plan to add to Victoria's road network


Context

This policy would add to Victoria’s road network by building, duplicating and upgrading roads, bridges
and intersections. In doing so it interacts with the Liberal Party of Australia – Victorian Division election
policy costing Ten Billion in Ten Years on Road Maintenance.

The Ten Billion in Ten Years on Road Maintenance costing would increase road maintenance and
captures the additional maintenance costs associated with the road infrastructure investment
stipulated in this policy.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪   $1,241.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

    -   an increase in assets of $1,514.1 million due to the construction of road projects

    -   an increase in operating expenses of $51.0 million due to feasibility studies and grants for road-
        related projects

    -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $324.2 million due to Australian Government
        funding

▪   $2,851.2 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

    -   an increase in assets of $3,731.7 million due to the construction of road projects

    -   an increase in operating expenses of $66.0 million due to feasibility studies and grants for road-
        related projects

    -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $946.5 million due to Australian Government
        funding.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $3,731.7 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2029–30.

For the capped cost components of this policy, the financial implications are certain as they relate to
specified capped funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve
the desired outcomes.




                                                                                                              4
                                                      OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing



Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. There would be no additional earthworks required beyond what is stipulated in Victorian Planning
   Authority benchmark costings.

2. There would be no relocation of utilities.

3. Partial land parcels would be acquired where necessary and plausible, otherwise entire properties
   would be acquired.

4. Land would be acquired based on the escalated median land value from the most recent property
   sales statistics published by the Valuer-General Victoria.

5. The Victorian Government would undertake all related works that are currently planned.

6. The characteristics of freeway and highway duplications and overpass construction would be
   consistent with similar projects.

7. Project start dates would be staggered to ensure works are manageable.

8. Projects with capped costing amounts would incorporate administrative costs.

9. The Western Highway project would undertake works consistent with the City of Melton’s
   recommendations.

10. There would be no contaminated land or material that would require removal.

11. Australian Government funding is provided for the following projects:

        a. $700.0 million to upgrade the Western Highway

        b. $125.0 million to duplicate Barwon Heads Road

        c. $71.5 to duplicate Jetty Road (with $3.5 million of funding already spent on the business case)

        d. $50.0 million to build the Wallan Diamond and upgrade Watson Street.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       determined the distance of road to be duplicated

▪       determined the number of new intersections, interchanges, overpasses, bridges and culverts to be
        built or upgraded

▪       determined the amount of land to be acquired

▪       estimated the costs of duplicating the road

▪       estimated the cost of building or upgrading new intersections, interchanges, overpasses, bridges
        and culverts




    5
                                                 OFFICIAL

                                                            An integrated plan to add to Victoria's road network


▪   estimated the value of land to be acquired

▪   estimated the timing of Australian Government funding.

We based the capped costing components of this policy on the capped amount of funding specified in
the election commitment.

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                   2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $15.4 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $24.5 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from planning, and undertaking an audit of the
V/Line fleet.

The financial implications of this policy for planning costs are certain as they relate to specified capped
funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired
outcomes.

This cost estimate of undertaking an audit of the V/Line fleet is sensitive to:

▪       the quantity of rolling stock

▪       the scope and complexity of the audit.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The audit would cover in service, storage or under repair passenger rolling stock assets.

2. Locomotives and historic rolling stock assets would be excluded from the audit.

3. Auditors can perform around or during train scheduling without service disruptions.

4. The quantity of rolling stock will remain at the current level.

5. The cost of the audit will be consistent with similar audits the Victorian Auditor General’s Office
   (VAGO) has undertaken, adjusted for complexity and number of assets in scope.

6. The cost of the audit would increase in line with Melbourne wage price index.

7. VAGO would agree to undertake the audit and receive additional funding.

8. This would be a one-off audit and VAGO would commence and complete the audit in 2023–24.

9. The specified funding amount for planning would encompass administrative costs.




    3
                                                 OFFICIAL

                                                  An integrated transport plan to add to Victoria's train network


Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined the current quantity of passenger rolling stock assets operated by V/Line

▪   estimated the unit cost to audit a single piece of rolling stock against disability requirements based
    on similar audits

▪   adjusted the unit cost to account for different levels of audit complexity across asset types

▪   estimated the fixed costs incurred to undertake the audit

▪   escalated the cost using the relevant indexation

▪   calculated the total cost to audit the entire V/Line fleet against disability requirements

▪   based planning costs on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election
    commitment.

Data sources

▪   Department of Transport. 2022. Victorian Rolling Stock Strategy. [ONLINE] Available at:
    https://transport.vic.gov.au/-/media/tfv-documents/rss.pdf. [Accessed 20 December 2022]

▪   Matthew Guy. 2022. Another day, another problem that’s shut down the entire regional train
    network. [ONLINE] Available at: https://www.matthewguy.com.au/media-releases/2022-05-31-
    another-day-another-problem-thats-shut-down-t. [Accessed 20 December 2022]

▪   Victorian Auditor-General's Office. 2020. Accessibility of Tram Services. [ONLINE] Available at:
    https://www.audit.vic.gov.au/report/accessibility-tram-services. [Accessed 20 December 2022]

Budget impact




Costing components

                                                                                                               2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $193.7 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $193.0 million due to building of tram infrastructure

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $1.0 million due to maintenance costs and operating
            additional tram services

        -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $0.3 million due to increased farebox revenue from
            additional services

▪       $347.9 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $295.8 million due to building of tram infrastructure

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $71.9 million due to maintenance costs and operating
            additional tram services

        -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $19.8 million due to increased farebox revenue from
            additional services

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $295.8 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2027–28.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in:

▪       final scope and design of tram infrastructure

▪       Melbourne consumer price index

▪       uptake of services.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Public Transport Victoria would absorb any costs incurred from routing and timetable changes.

2. No land acquisition would be required.

3. The Victorian Government would incur no cost for any loss of on-street parking revenue.

4. The construction profile would be in line with similar infrastructure projects.

5. Construction, maintenance, and operating costs would increase with the relevant indexation.

6. The number of services that currently operate on each route would remain the same.




    3
                                                   OFFICIAL

            An integrated transport plan to add to Victoria's tram network, including extending routes 3, 48 and 75


7. Public Transport Victoria would absorb any costs incurred from routing and timetable changes.

8. Existing bus lines on Doncaster Road would not be impacted during and after construction.

9. Existing bus lines on Burwood Highway would not be impacted during and after construction.

10. Due to the length of each extension, asset works would take:

    a. 2 years on the Route 3 extension

    b. 5 years on the Route 48 extension

    c. 5 years on the Route 75 extension.

11. Delivering a Victorian integrated transport plan would incur no additional costs.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined the length of tram track to be extended

▪   determined the intersections that would be upgraded

▪   determined the tram stops that would be constructed

▪   estimated the unit costs of tram track extension and tram stops, based on similar projects

▪   applied the unit costs to the length of extension and number of new tram stops

▪   estimated the maintenance operating costs based on similar infrastructure projects

▪   estimated the farebox revenue that would be generated from new tram services.

Data sources

▪   AECOM/PwC. 2016. IV options assessment – Supplement B: Cost and contribution assessment.
    [ONLINE] Available at: https://www.infrastructurevictoria.com.au/wp-
    content/uploads/2019/04/AECOM-PwC-Supplement-B-v2-1.pdf [Accessed 19 December 2022]

    December 2022]

    guides [Accessed 19 December 2022]

OFFICIAL




        Publicly-announced policy costing

Cap local public transport fares to $2 full-fare $1 concession fare

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement            1 January 2023

Policy conclusion              30 June 2027

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to,
                               enacting a 4-and-a-half-year policy to cap daily maximum public
                               transport fares at:
                               ▪   $2 for full fare paying passengers
                               ▪   $1 for concession fare paying passengers.
                               This policy would not include V/Line services.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or
                               budget update.
                                         OFFICIAL

                                       Cap local public transport fares to $2 full-fare $1 concession fare



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                       2
                                                     OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Victoria’s public transport system operates on a zone-based fare system:

▪       Zones 1 and 2 cover metropolitan Melbourne – roughly a 50 km radius from the central business
        district.

        -   The daily cap for travel for a full fare-paying patron is $9.20 and the concession fare is $4.60.

        -   This applies to train, tram and bus travel.

▪       Zones 3 to 15 cover parts of regional and rural Victoria.

        -   The bus full fare cap is $7.20 and the concession fare is $3.60.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $1,001.3 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $1,439.4 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is due to a decrease in revenue from the reduction in public transport prices, partially offset by an
increase in revenue from increased demand for public transport travel.

This cost estimate is sensitive to fare price changes and patronage demand.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Post COVID-19, public transport revenue would reach around 80% of pre-COVID-19 levels by 2026–
   27 and thereafter increase in line with the Victorian budget’s population and consumer price index
   projections (trend).

2. This policy would not be affected by increases in capital and operating costs for the public
   transport system resulting from trend increases in patronage.

3. Peak patronage in 2018–19 represents the capacity of the network.

4. Increased patronage resulting from the change in fare price is capped at 2018–19 peak-period
   levels and therefore there is no increase in capital or operating costs of the public transport
   system.

5. Increased patronage resulting from the change in fare price affects full fare patronage first and
   then concession fare patronage.

6. Additional patrons take 2 trips per day on average.

7. Concession and full-fare patronage proportions would remain constant.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                              Cap local public transport fares to $2 full-fare $1 concession fare


8. Metropolitan public transport patronage responds as price elasticity estimations suggest.

9. There is no metropolitan modal substitution effect as the price of all public transport modes are
   changing by the same amount.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated the impact of COVID-19 on public transport usage and the rate of patronage return in
    following years

▪   estimated the revenue foregone from existing patrons from a reduction in public transport

▪   estimated the additional patronage resulting from a reduction in fare prices

▪   estimated the revenue increase from new patrons using public transport due to reduced fare prices

▪   escalated the revenue foregone by relevant indexation.

We applied a part-year impact to the first year of this policy to reflect a proposed 1 January 2023
commencement date.

Data sources

▪   Australian Bureau of Statistics. 2022. Fewer Australians using public transport after COVID-19.
    [ONLINE] Available at: https://www.abs.gov.au/media-centre/media-releases/fewer-australians-
    using-public-transport-after-covid-19. [Accessed 19 December 2022]

▪   Elsevier. Evidence of a post-COVID change in travel behaviour – Self-reported expectations of
    commuting in Melbourne. 2021. [ONLINE] Available
    at: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0965856421002391?via%3Dihub.
    [Accessed 19 December 2022]

▪   Information provided by the Department of Transport

▪   Public Transport Victoria. 2019. 2018–19 Annual Report. [ONLINE] Available
    at: https://www.ptv.vic.gov.au/assets/PTV-default-site/footer/data-and-reporting/annual-
    report/PTVH4043-Annual_Report_2019-A4-v1_ONLINE.pdf. [Accessed 19 December 2022]

▪   Public Transport Victoria. 2019. 2021–22 Annual Report. [ONLINE] Available
    at: https://transport.vic.gov.au/about/our-annual-report-2021-2022. [Accessed 19 December 2022]

▪   Public Transport Victoria. 2022. Growing Our Rail Network 2018–2025 - Public Transport Victoria.
    [ONLINE] Available at: https://www.ptv.vic.gov.au/footer/legal-and-policies/growing-our-rail-
    network-2018-2025/. [Accessed 19 December 2022]

▪   Transport Victoria. 2022. Fares - Public Transport Victoria. [ONLINE] Available
    at: https://www.ptv.vic.gov.au/tickets/fares/. [Accessed 19 December 2022]

▪   The CIE. 2020. Estimating the social marginal cost of public transport in Victoria. [ONLINE]
    Available at: https://www.thecie.com.au/publications-archive/estimating-the-social-marginal-cost-
    of-public-transport-in-victoria. [Accessed 19 December 2022]




                                                                                                              4
                                                                         OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing

        Publicly-announced policy costing

Frankston and Cranbourne rail extensions

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement            1 July 2023

Policy conclusion              Ongoing

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to:
                               ▪   extend the Frankston line to Baxter
                               ▪   extend the Cranbourne line to Clyde.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or
                               budget update.
                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Frankston and Cranbourne rail extensions



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                       2
                                                      OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Frankston line extension to Baxter

The Stony Point line currently operates V/Line services and is single track between Frankston and
Stony Point. There is 8 km of track between Frankston and Baxter on this line.

The Australian Government committed $225 million to the electrification of the track between
Frankston and Baxter in the lead up to the 2018 Victorian general election. It continues to commit this
amount and will release funding after a full business case is completed.

Cranbourne line extension to Clyde

The Cranbourne line runs from the Melbourne central business district to Cranbourne. Trains used to
run to Clyde on the South Gippsland line until the government closed that section of track in 1993. The
track still exists in parts but would need to be replaced to allow for metropolitan trains.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $1,104.9 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets $1,256.7 million due to rail and road infrastructure construction

        -   an increase in revenue of $151.8 million due to funding from the Australian Government

▪       $2,002.5 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets $1,903.8 million due to rail and road infrastructure construction

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $445.0 million due to maintenance of rail and road
            infrastructure and operation of new passenger services, partially offset by

        -   an increase in revenue of $346.3 million due to funding from the Australian Government and
            farebox revenue from new train services.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $1,903.8 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2027–28.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       the final design and profile of the project

▪       changes in forecast escalation of rail and road project costs

▪       changes in funding commitments from the Australian Government

▪       changes in uptake of new services.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                   Frankston and Cranbourne rail extensions



Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

Baxter electrification

1. The project would be of similar scope (excluding bus infrastructure) to the Baxter electrification
   option in the Baxter Electrification preliminary business case, prepared by the Level Crossing
   Removal Authority, on behalf of the Department of Transport, for the Australian Government.

2. There would be 6 level crossing removals as part of the extension.

3. Leawarra Station would be removed and replaced with a station at Frankston South closer to
   Frankston Hospital.

4. Australian Government funding allocation would use the same profile of the electrification works.

5. The volume of new metropolitan services between Frankston and Baxter would be consistent with
   the current volume of services on the Frankston line.

6. The existing V/Line train services between Frankston and Baxter would cease, with V/Line train
   services continuing between Baxter and Stony Point.

Clyde extension

7. No land acquisition would be required.

8. Consistent with similar track extension projects and rail construction work:

    a. Additional stations would be required along the length of the extension. In this project, there
       would be new stations at Cranbourne East and Clyde.

    b. Grade separations would be required on 6 roads between Cranbourne Station and a new station
       at Clyde. Road over rail separations were selected where possible to minimise cost.

    c. The existing Cranbourne Station would be upgraded.

    d. A stabling yard would be constructed at the end of the line, in Clyde.

9. The volume of services between Cranbourne and Clyde would be consistent with the current
   volume of services on the Cranbourne line.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined the distance of track to be extended and the location of new stations to be constructed

▪   determined the appropriate grade separation required for each of the roads that would intersect
    with the new rail extensions

▪   estimated the unit costs of constructing a rail extension, based on similar projects

▪   applied the unit cost to the extensions from Frankston to Baxter, and from Cranbourne to Clyde




                                                                                                         4
                                                     OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


▪       allocated the Australian Government funding as revenue and determined the profile of when the
        funding would be received

▪       estimated the costs of line-specific upgrades that would be required

▪       estimated the maintenance operating costs based on similar infrastructure operating costs

▪       estimated the costs of operating new metropolitan train services to Baxter and to Clyde

▪       estimating cost savings from removing V/Line services between Frankston and Baxter

▪       estimated the additional fare revenue from operating new metropolitan train services to Baxter and
        Clyde.

Geelong metro

Political party                Liberal Nationals Victoria
Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed $164.8

                               ▪   introduce new commuter services from Geelong to Colac,
                                   Bannockburn, and Werribee
                               ▪   build new train stations at Bell Post Hill and Bannockburn

                               ▪   build a new siding and turnout track at Bannockburn

                                   schedules

                               Asset works totalling $138.6 million would be completed by the end

                                       OFFICIAL
                                                  Geelong metro



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                            2
                                                    OFFICIAL
Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Currently, Sprinter railcars are used for shorter shuttle services in and between the following areas
across Victoria:
▪       Between Marshall, South Geelong and Geelong
▪       Ballarat and Bacchus Marsh
▪       Kyneton and Sunbury
▪       Seymour
▪       Traralgon
▪       Frankston and Stony Point.
This policy would relocate each of these services to the Geelong area.
The Yelta line is a freight line between Geelong and Mildura. Currently, the track is broad gauge between
Geelong and Maryborough. Therefore, no trains would need to be regauged to operate services to
Bannockburn.
Since 2016, services from Melbourne to Geelong have used the Regional Rail Link (RRL). The RRL
bypasses the Werribee line, and residents in Werribee must travel to a station on the RRL in order to get
to Geelong.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $148.8 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:
        -   an increase in assets of $138.6 million due to building and upgrading stations, track works, and
            purchasing additional rollingstock
        -   an increase in operating expenses of $10.2 million due to maintenance costs, operating
            additional services and planning with local bus operators
▪       $164.8 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:
        -   an increase in assets of $138.6 million due to building and upgrading stations, track works, and
            purchasing additional rollingstock
        -   an increase in operating expenses of $26.2 million due to maintenance costs, operating
            additional services and planning with local bus operators
The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $138.6 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24 to
completion in 2024–25.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.

                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Halve V/Line fares



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                 2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Victoria’s public transport system operates on a zone-based fare system, with Zones 3 to 15 covering
parts of regional and rural Victoria. Peak period V/line train and coach services daily fare caps range
between $24.80 and $76.80. Peak period concession daily fare caps range between $12.40 and $38.40.
Off-peak period V/line train and coach services daily fare caps are 70% of the peak period price.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $149.2 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $211.5 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to a decrease in revenue from a reduction in V/Line fare prices and the provision of
free bus transport for V/Line patrons affected by unscheduled service disruptions, partially offset by an
increase in revenue from higher V/Line patronage due to lower fare prices.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in fare prices and patronage demand.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Post COVID-19, public transport revenue would reach an estimated 80% of pre-COVID-19 levels by
   2026–27 and thereafter increase in line with the Victorian budget’s population and consumer price
   index projections (trend).

2. Increases in capital and operating costs for the public transport system resulting from trend
   increases in patronage do not affect this policy.

3. Peak patronage in 2018–19 represents the capacity of the network.

4. Increased patronage resulting from the change in fare price is capped at 2018-19 peak-period levels
   and therefore there is no increase in capital or operating costs of the public transport system.

5. Increased patronage resulting from the change in fare price affects full fare patronage first and
   then concession fare patronage.

6. Additional patrons take 2 trips per day on average.

7. The concession and full-fare patronage proportions would remain constant.

8. Regional public transport patronage responds as price elasticity estimations suggest.

9. V/Line rail and bus services have comparable elasticities and fare prices.

10. Journeys by additional patrons as a result of the reduced fares would reach the daily fare cap.




    3
                                                 OFFICIAL

                                                                                         Halve V/Line fares


11. Unscheduled V/Line disruptions account for half of all service reliability issues.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated the impact of COVID-19 on public transport usage and the rate of patronage return in
    following years

▪   estimated the revenue foregone from existing patrons through a reduction in public transport fare
    prices

▪   estimated the additional patronage resulting from a reduction in fare prices

▪   estimated the revenue increase from new patrons using public transport because of the reduction
    in fare prices

▪   escalated the revenue foregone by relevant indexation

▪   estimated the proportion of V/Line services affected by unscheduled disruptions

▪   estimated the impact of providing free bus travel for patrons affected by unscheduled V/Line
    service disruptions.

We applied a part-year impact to the first year of this policy to reflect a proposed 1 January 2023
commencement date.

Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                        2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Suburban Rail Loop (SRL) is a multi-stage infrastructure project that the Victorian Government first
announced in 2018. The government plans to deliver the SRL in 3 sections over several decades:

1. SRL East – Cheltenham to Box Hill

2. SRL North – Box Hill to Melbourne Airport

3. SRL West – Melbourne Airport to Werribee.

Planning and initial works for the project commenced in 2019–20, with construction of SRL East
commencing in 2022. The government expects SRL East to cost between $30–$34.5 billion, with first
trains running in 2035.

The Victorian Government has committed $11.8 billion funding for the SRL to date, across multiple
budgets, and the Australian Government has committed $2.2 billion for SRL East construction works
over the next 5 years. The Prime Minister has stated that this funding cannot be used for any other
purpose.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $3,648.3 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   a decrease in assets of $3,671.5 million due to savings from ceasing SRL works

        -   a decrease in expenses of $953.3 million due to savings from ceasing SRL works

        -   partially offset by a decrease in revenue of $976.5 million due to forgone Australian
            Government funding

▪       $10,248.8 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   a decrease in assets of $10,568.9 million due to savings from ceasing SRL works

        -   a decrease in expenses of $1,879.9 million due to savings from ceasing SRL works

        -   partially offset by a decrease in revenue of $2,200.0 million due to forgone Australian
            Government funding.

We expect that the Australian Government’s $2.2 billion commitment is additional to the Victorian
Government’s $11.8 billion commitment. We expect that without this policy, the Australian Government
contribution would be used for additional works beyond those already committed to by the Victorian
Government.

This policy, to immediately shelve the SRL, would result in the Australian Government contribution
being returned to the Australian Government and a budget saving to the Victorian Government
recovering the unspent and uncommitted elements of its $11.8 billion commitment.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       the terms and conditions of SRL-related contracts

▪       the disbursement profile of Australian Government funding.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                 Immediately shelve the Suburban Rail Loop



Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Funds already spent would not be recoverable.

2. No substantive SRL East main works contracts would be entered into before policy
   commencement.

3. All SRL East main works project funding is for asset initiatives.

    a. As the project progresses, the Victorian Government would continue to reclassify a portion of
       expenditure as operating instead of capital.

4. The Department of Transport would subsume the Suburban Rail Loop Authority and retain its
   assets.

    a. Machinery of government changes would be implemented on 1 January 2023.

    b. All Suburban Rail Loop Authority staff would receive full retrenchment benefits.

5. The Victorian Government budget includes the Australian Government funding commitment of
   $2.2 billion for SRL East construction.

    a. Australian Government funding would be used for additional works not previously budgeted for,
    expanding the total estimated investment of this project.

    b. The Australian Government funding disbursement profile would match SRL East main works
       expenditure.

6. The Australian Government would not transfer committed SRL East construction funding to any
   other Victorian Government project.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined the amount of funding committed to the project in the budget

▪   determined the cost and profile of delivering the project

▪   estimated the amount of funds already spent

▪   estimated the value of works already contracted

▪   estimated the cost of retrenchment benefits for all Suburban Rail Loop Authority staff

▪   calculated the amount of funds the policy would save.

                                                  OFFICIAL
Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Mode Shift Incentive Scheme (MSIS) aimed to reduce truck congestion on roads and ports by
shifting containerised freight from road to rail.
The Australian Road Research Board (ARRB) collaborates with all levels of government and private
sector organisations on road strategy. In 2022, the ARRB began expanding its work across all modes of
transport and transitioning into the National Transport Research Organisation (NTRO).

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $17.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26
▪       $22.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33
This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from funding the MSIS and the gold standard
freight network.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach
Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:
1. The specified funding amount would encompass administrative costs.
2. MSIS funding would be split evenly across the duration of the policy.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding specified in the election commitment.

                                                                      2
                                                     OFFICIAL
Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Road Safety Committee is a former Joint Investigatory Committee of the Parliament of Victoria. In
April 2015, it merged with the Law Reform, Drugs and Crime Prevention Committee and became the Law
Reform, Road and Community Safety Committee.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $51.1 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:
        -   an increase in operating expenses of $40.1 million from providing resources for roadside drug
            tests and operating the virtual driver training program
        -   an increase in assets of $11.0 million from fitting out buses for the driver training program
▪       $66.9 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:
        -   an increase in operating expenses of $55.9 million from providing resources for roadside drug
            tests and operating the virtual driver training program
        -   an increase in assets of $11.0 million from fitting out buses for the driver training program.
The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $11.0 million from works in 2023–24.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach
Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed the specified funding amount would encompass administrative
costs.

Method

When costing this policy, we
▪       accepted the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election commitment
▪       estimated the cost to provide additional roadside drug tests and assured the specified cost.

        Publicly-announced policy costing

Ten billion in ten years on road maintenance

Political party                Liberals and Nationals

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Matthew Guy MP

Policy commencement            1 July 2023

Policy conclusion              30 June 2033

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Matthew Guy MP committed to
                               increase investment in road maintenance to $10.0 billion over the
                               coming decade, ensuring $1.0 billion per year for the department's
                               Road Asset Management output.

Election commitment            https://pbo.vic.gov.au/response/2500

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or
                               budget update.
                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Ten billion in ten years on road maintenance



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                           2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Road Asset Management is an output in the Victorian Budget that delivers programs and initiatives to
maintain Victoria’s freeways and arterial roads. In the 2022–23 budget, Road Asset Management
output was allocated $592.7 million for 2022–23, with expected road maintenance output and asset
expenditure of $780.0 million.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $1,099.2 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $2,886.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from increasing funding for road asset
management.

The financial implications of this policy are sensitive to changes in:

▪       future spending on road asset management

▪       the Victorian wage price index (WPI).




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed that current funding for road asset management output would
increase annually by the Victorian WPI.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       estimated current yearly funding for road asset management

▪       calculated the total increase in yearly funding by deducting the current budgeted road asset
        management amounts from the amount of funding specified in the election commitment.

Data sources

▪       Victorian Budget 2022–23, Budget Paper No. 3 Service Delivery 2022–23
